===== PAGE 1 =====
The Yang-Mills measure on surfaces via Morse theory
R. Chhaibi, N.V. Dang, Y. Guedes Bonthonneau, G. Rivière and T.D. Tô
July 28, 2026
Abstract
We introduce a Morse theoretical approach to the construction of the Yang–Mills
measure on the space of connections of a compact Riemannian surface. This provides a
direct continuous version of this measure which was previously obtained through lattice
approximations by Chevyrev in the case of the flat torus and by one of the authors and
Nohra for general compact Riemannian surfaces.
The starting point is the new notion of a Morse gauge together with the resolution of
random cohomological equations associated to Morse–Smale vector fields. This is achieved
by improving exponential convergence to equilibrium results for Morse–Smale gradient
flows that were obtained by two of the authors in the context of the study of Ruelle spectra
and by Jia, Stewart and Sverak in the context of simplified models from fluid mechanics.
CombiningtheserandomsolutionswiththedatagivenbytheMorsecomplex, weintroduce
a free Yang-Mills measure on space of connections and, using classical tools from stochastic
differential equations, we show how to make sense of holonomies for random connections
along a large class of curves.
Finally, by setting a proper conditioning of this free measure through these random
holonomies, we define the Yang–Mills measure and we compute its partition function
together with the law of random holonomies with respect to this measure, recovering the
formulas from the works of Migdal, Witten and Lévy.

1 Introduction
Let(Σ,h)be a smooth (C ∞), compact, connected and oriented Riemannian surface and let
Gbe a connected and compact linear Lie group with Lie algebrag. A smooth connection∇
on thetrivial bundleP:= Σ×G→Σcan be identified with a smooth one-formAthat is
g-valued, i.e.∇=d+AwithA∈Ω 1(Σ,g). The curvature of the connection is then defined
as
F(A) =dA+A∧A∈Ω 2(Σ,g),
and it gives rise to the so-called Yang–Mills functional on the space of connections:
SYM(A) := 1
2
∫
Σ
∥⋆hF(A)∥ 2
gdυ,
whereυis the Riemannian volume associated withh,⋆ h : Ω2(Σ,g)→C ∞(Σ,g)is the Hodge
star map and where∥.∥g is a norm1 ongthat is invariant under the adjoint representation,
Adg(a) = gag−1,(g,a)∈G×g. The classical Yang–Mills theory is concerned among other
things with the study of the critical points of this functional which is a classical and difficult
topic in nonlinear partial differential equations (especially in dimension4). In dimension2,
it is a celebrated result of Atiyah and Bott [5] that this functional is a perfectG-equivariant
Morse function with infinitely many critical values that can be computed explicitly. In fact,
one of the key features of this problem is its invariance by gauge transformations. The action
ofg∈C ∞(Σ,G)on connections by gauge transformation is indeed defined by the map
∇↦→∇g := g∇g−1 ⇔A↦→Ag := gAg−1−(dg)g−1,
and one hasF(A g) = gF(A)g −1.In particular, for allg∈C ∞(Σ,G), one hasS YM(A) =
SYM(Ag)and critical points come into families.
Besides studying this functional, other quantities playing a central role to describe spaces
ofconnectionsaretheso-calledholonomies. Indeedthesegeometricobjectsallowustoidentify
these spaces (modulo gauge choice) as subsets ofG–valued morphisms on the space of loops
ofΣ(modulo conjugation byG) – see [57, 60] for details. These holonomies are defined as
follows. Given aC1 curveγ: [0,1]→Σ, one can then define the parallel transport of∇along
γby solving the following ordinary differential equation:
dgγ(t)
dt + gγ(t)Aγ(t)(γ′(t)) = 0,g γ(0) =Id G.(1.1)
The holonomy alongγis then defined asHol γ(A) := gγ(1)∈G. These geometric quantities
turn out to be central when considering the quantum Yang–Mills theory where one aims at
defining a proper path integral. Recall that Yang–Mills theory appeared in the 1950s as a
gauge-theoretical foundation for the strong and weak nuclear forces in physics and that it has
since become a standard topic in mathematical physics. As for the classical theory, the case
of quantum Yang–Mills in dimension4where one aims at constructing a non-perturbative,
continuum Yang–Mills measure consistent with the Osterwalder–Schrader axioms is a noto-
rious difficult problem. Even the case of dimension3is still not fully understood despite
1The reader may keep in mind the example whereG= SU(N)and the scalar product we shall use on the
Lie algebrasu(N) =g=T IdSU(N)is given by the trace∥B∥ 2 := Tr (B∗B) =−Tr(B 2),sinceB ∗ =−Bfor
anti-Hermitian matrices.

===== PAGE 5 =====
significant progress that was obtained using stochastic quantization. Yet, as in the classical
theory, the2-dimensional case is much better understood and the probabilistic constructions
of this Quantum Field Theory (QFT) has a rich history at the crossroads of mathematics and
physics starting from the seminal works of Migdal [63].
On the quantum side and from the mathematical perspective, one is in fact interested in
giving proper mathematical sense to the following formal measure on the space of connections
and for a large class of test functionsΦ:
∫
A/G
Φ(A)e−SYM(A)dA,(1.2)
whereA/Gis a subspace of connections inD ′(Σ,T∗Σ⊗g)modulo the action of the gauge
groupG. In particular, the caseΦ = 1corresponds to the so-calledpartition functionin
Quantum Field Theory and one expects more generally to encompass geometric relevant test
functions of the formΦ(A) = Ψ(Holγ(A))in view of computing the law of holonomies along
a given pathγ. This means that one should be able to define holonomies along curves even
ifAmay have low regularity almost surely. To circumvent these issues, this question was
tackled by defining a measure on the space ofG-valued morphisms on spaces of loops thanks
to the above identification through holonomies. In fact, early works by Gross, King, and
Sengupta [42], by Driver on the plane [32] and later by Sengupta on general surfaces [73]
constructed this measure as a gauge-invariant random holonomy law. A complementary
viewpoint, developed in particular by Lévy [57] on two-dimensional Markovian holonomy
fields, emphasizes the structure of the Yang–Mills measure as a Markov process indexed by
loops, with transition kernels given by heat kernels on the structure groupG. Lévy’s work
provides a deep understanding of the Wilson loop observables and the Makeenko–Migdal
equations from a probabilistic and geometric perspective.
However, a more analytic question remained open for some time: can one construct the
Yang–Mills measuredirectlyas a randomdistributional connection, i.e., as a probability mea-
sure on a space ofg-valued distributional1-forms? This is subtle because one has to fix a
proper gauge otherwise the measure does not look absolutely continuous with respect to any
free field measure. Moreover, one knows from standard properties of the white noise that
such connections have their curvature lying inHκ for everyκ <−1whereH κ denotes the
standard Sobolev scale. In particular, the action functional is infinite on this rough set of
fields and holonomies are also hard to define. For the flat torusT2, Chevyrev recently solved
these problems and he achieved this construction by defining a Yang–Mills measure as scaling
limit of some lattice gauge model from discrete Yang–Mills theory [19]. Even more recently
and relying on the Morse gauge which is introduced in the present work, Nohra and one
of the authors have pushed this program further [24]. In this reference, they construct the
Yang–Mills measure as a random distributional1-form on compact surfaces of arbitrary genus
equipped with an arbitrary smooth area form as scaling limit of discrete connections com-
ing from lattice gauge theory onΣ. Actually, they prove the following universality theorem:
their continuum Yang–Mills measure arises as the scaling limit of a wide class of lattice gauge
theories (including Wilson, Manton, and Villain actions) on any compact surface.
The present work generalizes Chevyrev’s result to arbitrary closed surfacesΣof genus
g≥0, complementing and extending in other directions the results of [24]. Indeed, while
[24] focuses on universality from lattice approximations, we use dynamical systems methods
to provide a direct and self-contained continuum construction of the Yang–Mills measure as
5

===== PAGE 6 =====
a random distributional connection without passing through a lattice limit. Our approach
requires overcoming several obstacles that will be described precisely later on. Among others,
we constructa novel global and singular Morse gaugeon a general surface and prove it ad-
dresses the issue of Gribov copies. Using the Morse complex, we also give a careful treatment
of the cohomological zero-modes which contains information on the topology of the moduli
space of flat connections appearing in Witten’s seminal work [80]. An informal summary of
our main results is as follows.
Informal main Theorem.Givena∈Σ, there exists a measureµ YM supported in
H−1−
loc (Σ\{a},T ∗Σ⊗g)whose partition function verifies Witten’s formula [80] and for which
one can define and compute laws of random holonomies for a large class of piecewiseC1
curves.
For the sake of consistency, we will in fact compute explicitly the laws of these random
holonomies for a large class of small loops and we will verify that they satisfy Migdal’s formu-
las [63, 57]. The computation along more general curves would require heavier combinatorial
work that we do not pursue here. We emphasize that the proof given in the present work is
self-contained and in particular completely independent of the results and of the lattice gauge
approach from [24] even if both works rely on the same choice of a Morse gauge. To the best
of our knowledge, [24] and the present work provide the first construction of the Yang–Mills
measure on an arbitrary closed surface as a random distributional connection. The fact that
we need to fix a base pointaon the surface comes from our construction through Morse
theory and from the existence of an attractor for the induced gradient dynamics. In view of
constructing the Yang–Mills measure, we will in fact make use of tools and ideas from Morse
theory and the theory of hyperbolic dynamical systems that we will combine with methods
from probability. Morse theory will be used to fix a dynamical gauge, a key ingredient for
defining the Yang–Mills measure. More precisely, we will proceed in three main steps of
different nature and of independent interest:
1.Classical Yang–Mills theory in2dthrough the lens of hyperbolic dynamical
systems. In this first step, we will revisit some aspects of the classical Yang–Mills
theory through the lens of dynamical systems theory. We will explain how to put the
Yang–Mills functional into a normal form using a Morse–Smale gradient flow onΣ
associated with a Morse function whose maximum is reached at the pointa. We refer
to this step as defining aMorse gaugeon the space of connections. Among other things,
we will show how to express a connection in terms of its curvature and of the resolvent
of the corresponding gradient vector field. This is achieved by building on ideas arising
in the study of Ruelle spectra in hyperbolic dynamical systems and by adapting there
some ideas that were initially used in the context of certain models from fluid mechanics
by Jia, Stewart and Sverak. The use of these tools from dynamical systems and PDE is
one of the main novelties of the present work both in the classical and in the quantum
parts.
2.Solving random cohomological equations in2d. Given ag-valued white noise
onΣ, we show how to solve random cohomological equations associated with these
Morse–Smale vector fields. We then prove that the random solutions that we obtain
can be integrated along a large class of curvesγand that they give rise to ag-valued
6

===== PAGE 7 =====
reparametrized Brownian motion. This allows us to define stochastic analogues of (1.1)
that can be solved using the standard theory of stochastic differential equations.
3.Quantum Yang–Mills theory in2d. With these tools at hand, we define first a free
boundary Yang–Mills measure by using the solutions to these random cohomological
equations and a correction through the associated Morse complex to take into account
the topology ofΣ. We explain how to make sense of random holonomies along a large
class of curves and we use holonomies around small loops surroundingato couple the
Gaussian part of our measure with the correction given by the Morse complex. Since
the coupling is nonlinear, this allows us to define a Yang–Mills measure which isnon
Gaussianand whose partition function can be computed explicitly and verifies Wit-
ten’s formula. Finally, we illustrate our construction by computing the law of random
holonomies along small loops of the surface and we show that they verify the formulas
appearing in Lévy’s works.
2 Main results
We now describe in more detail the main results pertaining the three main issues discussed
at the end of the introduction and leading to the continuous construction of the Yang–Mills
measure via Morse theory. Recall that our goal in the present paper is twofold. On the one
hand, we introduce a novel gauge namedMorse gaugeand we explain in which sense this
gauge slices the spaceAof connections and provides a nice model for the orbit spaceA/Gof
connections modulo gauge choice. This concerns classical Yang–Mills theory. On the other
hand, we give an application of this new gauge to quantum Yang–Mills theory by constructing
a Gibbs measure on theinfinite dimensional orbit spaceA/G.
2.1 Classical Yang–Mills theory in2dthrough the lens of hyperbolic dy-
namical systems
We letf: Σ→Rbe a smooth (C ∞) Morse function which has exactly2g+ 2critical
points wheregis the genus ofΣ. The Morse assumption means that the critical points are
nondegenerate. Denoting by Crit(f) :={a j : 1⩽j⩽2g+ 2}the set of critical points, we
also suppose that
f(a 1)<f(a 2)<...<f(a 2g+1)<f(a 2g+2).
The points(aj)2≤j≤2g+1 are the saddle points off, equivalently its critical points of index1.

of each saddle point off:

Recall thatd[W u(a)] = 0and also from [27, Prop. 7.7] thatι V ([Wu(a)] = 0for every
critical pointaof index1. In particular, one has
ιV (A∞) = 0,
andA∞ is said to be in Morse gauge. We would like to point out that some closely related idea
ofdynamicalgaugealreadyappearedintheliteratureunderthenameofAnosovgauge[44,71],
we wonder if a similar result as Theorem 2.1 would make sense or could be established for
the Anosov gauge. We also emphasize thatA∞ has low regularity so that its curvature does
not a priori make sense due to the (ill-defined) quadratic termsA∞∧A∞. Despite that,
the fourth item shows that this quadratic term is formally concentrated at the maximum
off. This illustrates how singular the Morse gauge is and this is somewhat reminiscent of
the concentration of curvature phenomena discovered by Uhlenbeck in4–dimensional gauge
theories [31]. Observe also that the regularity statement in the third item is somehow sharp
in terms ofLp regularity asdA∞ /∈H−1. The fifth item ensures in some sense the existence
of the holonomy for the limit connection despite the low regularity ofA∞. Finally, the last
item tells us in which (weak) sense we can think ofA∞ as a choice of gauge forAin its gauge
orbit. In summary, this Theorem provides a global gauge for our problem at the expense of
losing the regularity ofAto take into account the topology ofΣand its proof is given in §5
building on the tools from §3 and §4.
More generally, any connection having the form given by equation (2.3) withβ∞∈ker(ι V )
is said to be in theMorse gauge. The main conceptual input of our work is in fact the
introduction of this gauge which is a far reaching generalization of the classical axial gauge
to general surfaces. It slices the spaceAof connections and it provides a nice model of the
orbit spaceA/G. Moreover, this gauge gives in some sense a normal form for the Yang–
Mills functional and any connection in the Morse gauge decomposes as a sum of some part
that contributes to the functional which contains all the curvature contribution and some
zero modes partof the connection. The zero modes part is supported on unstable curves and
already appeared in the representations of the Morse complex in terms of currents [55, 45, 28].
An important remark about why our gauge fixing does not suffer from the problem of Gribov
ambiguitiesisthatour limit gauge transformations are discontinuousalongthewedgeofcircles
∪2⩽j⩽2g+1Wu(aj). Therefore the classical Gribov argument yielding a topological obstruction
to global gauge fixing no longer applies since it relies on calculating homotopy groups of
the groupC 0(Σ,G)of continuous gauge transformations. An important feature of the Morse
gauge is as follows: if the connection∇=d+Awe started with is flat, thenF ∞ = 0and
the connectionA∞ isconcentratedon the union of unstable curves. This is a non abelian
analogue of the quasi–isomorphism between the de Rham complex of smooth forms and the
Morse complex of currents as shown in the works of Laudenbach [55].
As we shall see, the existence of the limit curvatureF∞ follows from elementary geometric
and analytic considerations but it only provides limited regularity. Hence, the convergence
of the integral term (as well as that ofAT) in the second item of our theorem requires a
very careful analysis. IfF ∞ was smooth (or at leastCk withklarge enough), this would
follow from the spectral analysis of gradient flows as it was developed by two of the authors
in [27]. In order to overcome this regularity issue, we will refine the results from this reference
by adapting some ideas that were used by Jia, Stewart and Sverak [50] in the context of
certain simplified models from fluid mechanics (the so-called De Gregorio equation). This is
9

===== PAGE 10 =====
the content of Theorem 4.1 below which is the main analytical statement behind this first
theorem and its upcoming probabilistic version, namely Theorem 2.2. Roughly speaking,
Theorem 4.1 is an exponential decay of correlations result for the free transport equation
associated with (2.1) and stated in a sharp families of weighted Lebesgue spaces. Gradient
flows are in fact part of the larger family of Axiom A dynamical systems as it was primarily
defined by Smale in the 1960s [77]. The spectral study of Axiom A systems at work here
has a long and well-established tradition going back to the works of Bowen and Ruelle in the
1970s [11, 6] and revisited during the last thirty years using sophisticated tools from functional
analysis. We refer the reader to [7, 30, 56] for books describing these recent progress. Finally,
we note that we will say very little about the convergence ofgT. Following the methods
from [46, 27], we could probably show the convergence to some limitg∞ in a much stronger
sense than what we will claim.
2.2 Solving random cohomological equations in2d
Regarding the expression (2.3) of the Morse gauge and the formal definition (1.2) of the Yang–
Mills measure, it is natural to pickβ∞ by choosingF∞ being a white noise. Hence, we let
ξbe ag-valued white noise onΣand we denote by(Ω,B,P)the corresponding probability
space. Recall thatξis an element ofL 2(Ω,H−1−κ(Σ,g))for everyκ>0and that, for every
ψinC ∞(Σ,g), one has

See Section 6 for more details. In view of defining a random connection using the Morse
gauge, we are thus left with solving the following random cohomological equation
LV (A) =ξι V (υ).(2.4)
This is the content of our second main theorem:
Theorem 2.2(Random cohomological equations).With the above conventions and for every
κ>0, the sequence ∫T

functional spaces adapted to the spectral analysis ofLV (and more generally for Axiom A
systems) have to enjoy some anisotropic Sobolev regularity, meaning that the right hand
side of (2.4) should have positive Sobolev regularity in certain direction even if it may have
negative Sobolev regularity along others. Here,ξis not better thanH−1− almost surely and
we need to proceed in a different way to deal with this isotropic low regularity. One more
10

time, the key ingredient is the decay of correlation property given in Theorem 4.1 which can
be combined with probabilistic arguments. The resolution of cohomological equations is a
standard topic in dynamical systems but, to the best of our knowledge, nothing seems to be
known on the resolution of random cohomological equations. Hence, on top of its applications
to Yang-Mills theory, Theorem 2.2 also seems to provide the first example of resolution of
such random equations. The proof of this result is given in §6 and let us now explain how
it will allow us to pick connections at random and to build the Yang–Mills measure. Let us
also mention that a resolution of (2.4) in sharper anisotropic spaces of distributions is given
in [25] by Nohra and one of us.

which is an attractor for the gradient flow used to define our random connections. Despite

distributions and this requires us to introduceS(Σ\{a2g+2},T∗Σ⊗g)the space of smoothg-

construction, one can expect that our random connections have more regularity along the
flow lines and less in a transverse direction to the flow. This interesting and subtle question,
which is addressed in [25], requires substantial extra work that we will not discuss in this
article.

from the absence of any coupling between the components of the connectionAcoming from

More concretely, we will blow–up our surfaceΣata2g+2 yielding a surface with boundaryS
which is diffeomorphic toΣminus a disc (see e.g. Figure 2) and we will require thatHol(∂S)
is close to the identity. Indeed, one should think of random connectionsAunder the free
boundary Yang–Mills measureµfree
YM as connections living on the bordered surface∂Schosen
randomly under the Yang–Mills measure but without any constraint on the restriction ofA
on the boundary∂S. This blow-up procedure explains somehow the choices ofσ-algebras
appearing earlier.
In summary, we will construct the Yang–Mills measure on closed surfaces starting from
some free boundary Yang–Mills measure which lives on the blow–up of the initial surfaceΣ
at the maximum of some Morse functionf: this is the probability law of a Gaussian random
connection plus some independent singular connection supported by the union of unstable
curves of some Morse gradient flow. Then we recover the Yang–Mills measure on the closed
surfaceΣbynonlinear conditioningof the free boundary Yang–Mills measure in a way which
14

===== PAGE 15 =====
is strongly reminiscent of the work of Sengupta in [73]. We would like to insist on two facts:
first, the Yang–Mills measure we obtain on the closed surface after conditioning isno longer
Gaussian. And this non Gaussianity holds true on any surface, be it the sphere
S2 or the torusT 2. In fact, it is not even Gaussian for the Maxwell theory, i.e. the case
ofG=U(1). Secondly, our random connections on the closed surfaceΣcan come from
different topological types of bundlesoverΣ. We refer to section 11 for a detailed
discussion of this fact in theU(1)case where our measure defines random connections living
on line bundles of different Chern classes. Forgetting about the Morse gauge, our methods
through random holonomy processes also bear some strong similarities with the early works
of Lévy on this topic [57, Chapter 2]. Finally, we emphasize that, along our construction and
besides defining random holonomy processes for large class of curves, we will also define a
novel class of Yang–Mills observables
Wγ : Ω×G 2g→g,
which are indexed by curvesγbelonging to some general class of curves seemingly optimal in
terms of regularity and which are the random analogues of the classical variable
∫
γA.
2.4 Literature review on mathematical 2D gauge theory
In the physics literature, the study of the Yang–Mills measure in2dimensions goes back to
the work of Migdal [63] in 1975 on the plane or planar domains. His approach was discrete
and in the spirit of lattice gauge theories as described by Polyakov in [66]. His construction
was then extended to surfaces of any genus in the groundbreaking works of Rusakov [70]
and Witten [80, 81] with relation to many topics in mathematics such as the Reidemeister
torsion, volumes of character varieties, the Verlinde formula for conformal blocks and many
other mathematical topics [80] [81]. From the mathematical perspective, the probabilistic
construction of the Yang–Mills measure as aholonomy processwas studied by Driver [32],
Gross–King–Sengupta [42] onR2 and later generalized by Fine [37], Sengupta [73] and finally
by Lévy [57] on general surfaces. Except for the work of Lévy, these authors rely on the idea
that the Yang–Mills measure becomes Gaussian once we fix the axial gauge (this is also called
the temporal gauge in PDE theory) which is an old idea from physics which goes back to
the seminal works of Kummer [53] and Arnowitt–Fickler [4] in the 1960s. One of the nice
properties of the axial gauge is that we do not need ghosts to fix the gauge and quantize
the theory since the Fadeev–Popov determinant is a constant for the axial gauge. This is
reminiscent of our observation that, in our work, we formally make the following change of
variables:
∫
A/G
Φ(A)e−SYM(A)dA=
∫
Ω×G2g
Φ
(
L−1
V ιV (F) +
∑
a
log ba[Wu(a)]
)
e−∥F∥2
2 dFµG(db).
Hence, in some sense, we decide to normalize to1the Jacobian determinant of our change of
variables(F,b)↦→A.
In [57, 58], Lévy gave a unified construction of this measure as a very general type of
stochastic process on the space of loops taking the celebrated Driver–Sengupta formula as
starting point. In particular, the area Markov property plays a central and beautiful role in
Lévy’s approach in [58]. Recently, in a breakthrough work, Chevyrev was able to construct for
15

===== PAGE 16 =====
the first time a genuine Yang–Mills measuredirectlyon distributional connections on flatT2
asscalinglimitofdiscreterandomconnectionswhoseholonomyalsoobeytheDriver–Sengupta
formula. This is the most relevant result from the viewpoint of our work. This advance
stimulated a number of results from the stochastic partial differential equations community
to explore the stochastic quantization of gauge theories, due to Chandra–Chevyrev–Hairer–
Shen [21, 22], Cao–Chatterjee [15, 16], Bringmann–Cao [13, 14], Chevyrev–Shen [20], Shen–
Smith–Zhu [75], Shen–Zhu–Zhu [76] and many others.
Finally, we mention that a parallel work of the third author with E. Nohra [24] constructed
adiscrete versionof the Yang–Mills measure which is compatible with the lattice gauge
theory on the surfaceΣ. They show its convergence in the scaling limit to the continuum
measure of the present paper, proving that the measure constructed in the present paper
can be obtained as scaling limit of statistical models defined on the lattice, in the same
way as for the Ising model and theΦ4
2 andΦ 4
3 models studied in constructive quantum field
theory. In particular, the continuum measure constructed in the present paper is universal,
independent of the details of the lattice discretization. As a consequence, the result of [24]
together with the present work obtain a new intrinsic construction of the Yang–Mills measure,
independent of previous constructions in the literature, and prove the convergence of partition
functions on all compact surfaces4. In fact in [24], the approach is to resolve globally the
surface by some cylinder and using global system of fake polar coordinates onΣthat is called
pseudocoordinates. Then in [24, section 5.1], specially [24, Equation (5.2) p. 36], one can find
an alternative definition of the solutionAN =L −1
V (ξιV (υ))of the cohomological equation
expressed in terms of a Brownian sheet written in the pseudocoordinates, in the notations of
[24]:

2.4.1 Open problems
Here we make a tentative list of some open questions which are left over by our work and that
we believe would deserve further investigation:
1. Can we prove the Driver–Sengupta formula in full generality for our Yang–Mills measure
for arbitrary embedded graphs in our surface?
2. Clarify the relation between our measure and the one constructed in [24], they should
be the same and there is strong evidence for this but a simple direct proof is somehow
missing.
4This is done in [24].
5A complete proof is part of one of the open questions that we listed
16

===== PAGE 17 =====
3. What can we define in the bordered case or what should we do when there are multiple
boundary components?
4. Related to the previous question: can we prove a spatial Markov property and some
form of Segal gluing for our Yang–Mills measure?
5. What happens if we change the Morse function? This is an extremely natural question.
Then how do we compare the measures obtained under different choices of Morse–Smale
flows?
6. Can we condition our Yang–Mills random connection to be some connection on some
non trivial vector bundle of fixed topological type? The case of the abelian Yang–Mills
measure conditioned on nontrivialU(1)-bundles of fixed Chern number is described in
paragraph 11.3.
7. What happens for non compact groups? One should start with theSL2(R)case first.
8. Can we randomly choose our gauge fixing? Could we compare our measure with the
holonomy process constructed by T.Lévy [57]?
9. Can we put the connection in Morse gauge back in a different gauge? How does this
compare to the connection constructed by Chevyrev [19], if we could go back to the
Coulomb gauge could we recover the measures constructed by Chevyrev on the torus?
10. What happens on non orientable surfaces?
11. If∇fis not Morse–Smale, can we still solve the cohomological equations? In higher
dimension (d= 3,4) could we use the same techniques to construct Maxwell and abelian
Chern-Simons theories?
12. Could we study the critical values and critical points of the Yang–Mills functional in
the Morse gauge?
13. How can this construction help for SPDE construction ofYM 2 on general surfaces?
Could it be useful for constructing the Yang–Mills–Higgs theory on general surfaces?
14. Would our method be useful for the3dYang–Mills measure? For the moment, we
strongly doubt that our method would have any relevance for the construction ofYM3.
2.5 Organization of the article
In Section 3, we review some material from classical Morse theory and we fix some notations
that are used throughout the article. In Section 4, we show the main analytical result on
gradient flow. Namely, we prove a spectral gap estimate that will be used both in the proof
of Theorem 2.1 and 2.2. In Section 5, we give the proof of Theorem 2.1 using methods and
ideas from the classical theory of hyperbolic dynamical systems. Along the way, we also
describe families of classical observables. In Section 6, we define the white noise precisely
together with some of its elementary properties and we then prove Theorem 2.2. The longer
Section7describeslargefamiliesalongwhichweexpecttodefinerandomholonomiesandmore
general Yang–Mills observables. Then, we explain how to integrate the random solutions from
17

===== PAGE 18 =====
Theorem 2.2 along such curves and we show that the resulting process is a reparametrized
g-valued Brownian process. After that, in Section 8, we explain how to solve the stochastic
differential equations associated with theseg-valued Brownian processes and we prove some
basic properties of their solutions. Once all these tools are settled, we prove Theorems 2.3
and 2.4 in Section 9 except for the definition and the law of random holonomies that is
explained in Section 10. Section 11 is entirely devoted to a treatment of the abelian case where
we give details on the Morse gauge fixing for connections on non trivial line bundles. Then we
discuss how our Yang–Mills measure charges connections with different Chern numbers and
how one can condition our Yang–Mills measure to line bundles of fixed Chern class.
Finally, Appendix A is mostly intended for readers less familiar with stochastic differential
equations who will find here material and references on this material that is mostly used in
Section 8.

3 Preliminaries on Morse functions
LetΣbe aC ∞ compact surface which is oriented, connected and boundaryless. In this section,
we review some material on gradient vector fields generated by a Morse function and we fix
the conventions that are used throughout the article. The key ingredient in view of our
construction of the Yang-Mills measure is Theorem 3.4 which describes the convergence to
equilibrium for Morse-Smale gradient flows. This result will be further refined in Section 4 in
order to fit the analytical problems we will encounter when constructing the so-called Morse
gauge of a connection.
18

===== PAGE 19 =====
3.1 Morse functions
Letf: Σ→Rbe a smooth function. We say thatfis aMorse functionif all its critical points
are nondegenerate. It can be shown that the set of Morse functions is open and dense in the
C∞ topology. We denote the set of critical points offby Crit(f)and a Morse function is said
to be perfect if|Crit(f)|= 2g+ 2(wheregis the genus of the surfaceΣ)and if all the critical
values offare distinct. The set of perfect Morse functions is also open (and nonempty) in
theC ∞-topology.
A fundamental property of Morse functions is the so-called Morse Lemma:
Lemma 3.1(Morse Lemma).Letf: Σ→Rbe aC ∞ Morse function. Then, for anyain
Crit(f), there exists a smooth chart centered atasuch that, in these local coordinates, the
functionfreads
f(x) =f(a) + 1

(
ε1x2
1 +ε 2x2
2
)
,
withε 1,ε 2 ∈ {−1,1}. Ifε 1 =ε 2 = 1, we say thatahas index0(local minimum). If
ε1 = 1,ε 2 =−1, we say thatahas index1(saddle point). Ifε 1 =ε 2 =−1, we say thatahas
index2(local maximum).
With this Lemma at hand, we introduce the notion of locally flat metric near critical
points [45, §2]:
Definition 3.2.Letf: Σ→Rbe aC ∞ Morse function. We fix a Morse chart near every
point inCrit(f). We say that the metric˜his locally flat near critical points if it reads in the
local Morse coordinates

By a partition of unity argument, one can verify that such metrics exist.

2. near the saddle points off,
∇hff=x 1∂x1−x 2∂x2, φt
f(x1,x 2) = (etx1,e−tx2),(3.4)
3. near the minimum off,
∇hff=x 1∂x1 +x 2∂x2, φt
f(x1,x 2) = (etx1,etx2).(3.5)
One important fact is that the dynamics is linear in Morse charts. Given a critical point
aoff, we define the stable and unstable manifolds ofa:
Wu(a) :=
{

{

These are embedded submanifolds ofΣwhich are diffeomorphic toR2−ind(a) (forW u(a)) and
toR ind(a) (forW s(a)). As we are working with compact surfaces, one can verify that these
submanifolds induce de Rham currents, i.e.
∀ψ∈Ω 2−ind(a)(Σ),⟨[W u(a)],ψ⟩:=

[Wu(a2g+2)] =δ 0(x1,x 2)dx1∧dx 2; (2) near the saddle points(a j)2≤j≤2g+1,[W u(aj)] =
δ0(x2)dx2 and (3) near the minimuma1,[W u(a1)] = 1. In fact, asfhas a single minimum,
one has globally[Wu(a)] = 1in the case where ind(a) = 0.
Remark 3.3.All along the article, we adopt the following geometric convention. IfXdenotes

3.3 Convergence to equilibrium
A key property of gradient flows for our analysis is the following theorem showing convergence
to equilibrium for gradient flows:
Theorem3.4(Harvey-Lawson, Dang-Rivière).Letf: Σ→Rbe aC ∞ perfect Morse function
and leth f be aC∞ locally flat metric. Suppose that(f,h f)has the Morse-Smale property.
Then, there existsN0≥1such that, for every0≤k≤2, for every0<β <1and for every
ψ∈Ω k(Σ),
∀t≥0, φ −t∗
f (ψ) =
∑
a:ind(a)=k
(∫
Ws(a)
ψ
)
[Wu(a)] +OD′(e−tβ),
where the constant in the remainder depends only onβand∥ψ∥CN0.
The fact that one has convergence was proved by Harvey and Lawson in [46] while the rate
of convergence and a full asymptotic expansion were obtained by two of the authors [27, 28].
Note that this result holds true for much more general gradient flows but we restrict to the
setting of interest for our analysis. In the upcoming section, we will try to refine this result in
the case of forms of degree0(meaning functions) for which convergence follows in fact directly
from the dominated convergence Theorem. Our goal is to get more precise information on
the norms involved in the remainder term. For degree1, we will not need information on the
size of the remainder and we will directly use the original version of this convergence result
as in [46].
3.4 The case of Lie algebra valued forms
Inviewofourfutureapplications, webrieflydiscusshowTheorem3.4extendstomatrixvalued
forms. More precisely, we letGbe a compact Lie group. We denote bygthe corresponding
Lie algebra. Recall that there is a natural mappingAd: g∈G↦→Adg∈Aut(G)defined
asAd g(h) = ghg−1.The differential of this map at the identity elemente∈Ginduces a
Lie algebra automorphism denoted by Adg ∈Aut(g)(the so-called adjoint representation
ofG). All along the article, we will make the assumption thatGis a compactlinearLie
group, meaning that it is a compact subgroup of GLN(C). This implies that the adjoint
representation reads Adg(a) = gag−1. From now on, we will fix a norm∥.∥ g ongthat is
invariant under the adjoint representation.
Remark 3.5.According to [51, Cor. 4.22], any compact Lie group is isomorphic to a closed
linear group.
Example 3.6.In the case whereG= SU(N)withN≥1, one hasAd gX= gXg −1. A

4 Contraction on weightedL p-spaces
In this section, we aim at proving a somehow refined version of Theorem 3.4 in the case of
0-forms (meaning functions). More precisely, we will give a precise estimate on the remainder
appearing in this Theorem. To do this, we will follow an idea due to Jia, Stewart and Sverak
in the context of1-dimensional reductions of certain equations from fluid mechanics [50, §3].
Namely, givenγ >1andp≥1such that 2

we can at most visit eachPj,j∈{1,...,2g+ 2}exactly once.
Proof.Suppose that, given a pointx∈Σ, one can find integersk 1 < k2 < ... < kL such

In other words, the number of iterations where the trajectory exits the fixed neighborhoods
of the critical points is uniformly bounded in terms of the topology ofΣ(precisely by the
genus). By similar arguments, one has

ensure that they do not intersect the stable manifolds of the other saddle points. In particular,
all the points in one of thesePj will not enter the neighborhoods of the other saddle points
in forward time. Equivalently, all theαj withJ 0 + 1< j < J1−1are equal to some fixed
integer between2and2g+ 1and we can work in the Morse chart to estimate the volume of
this neighborhood.
For the rest of the article, we make the assumptionsthatfis aC∞ perfect Morse
function, that the pair(f,h f)has the Morse-Smale property and that the metrichf isC ∞
and locally flat near any critical point. In particular, Theorem 4.1 applies.

6 Random connections as solutions to random cohomological
equations
The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 2.2 which will directly follow from the slightly
more general statement from Theorem 6.6. To that aim, we first collect a few definitions
and properties of thewhite noiseon a compact Riemannian surface. Then, we explain how
to solve the random cohomological equation (2.4) with truncated white noise on the right
hand side as forcing term. Finally, we describe the independence properties of these random
solutions.
33

===== PAGE 34 =====

6.3 Independent random connections
For later applications, we conclude this section by discussing the independence properties
of our random connections when we pickψ1 =1 U1 andψ 2 =1 U2 withU 1 andU 2 disjoint
measurable subsets ofΣ. Indeed, one has
Lemma 6.7.LetU 1 andU 2 be two measurable subsets ofΣsuch that1 U11U2 = 0υ-almost
everywhere. Then, the random connectionsAU1 :=A 1U1 andA U2 :=A 1U2 from Theorem 6.6
are independent.
As in Remark 6.5, the proof of this result follows from the facts thatξU1 andξ U2 are
independent and thatιVL−1
V has well-behaved probabilistic properties. We just provide the
proof for the sake of completeness.
Proof.LetΨ 1,Ψ 2∈Ω 1(Σ), let1⩽k,ℓ⩽Land set
Z1,k :=
⟨
ιVL−1
V (1U1⟨ξ,bk⟩υ),Ψ 1
⟩
, Z 2,ℓ :=
⟨
ιVL−1
V (1U2⟨ξ,bℓ⟩υ),Ψ 2
⟩
.
Both are centered and depend linearly onξ, hence belong to the first Wiener chaos ofξ.
Any finite family of such variables is jointly Gaussian, so that independence is equivalent to
decorrelation and it suffices to computeE(Z1,kZ2,ℓ). Using that the Gaussian coefficients of
ξare independent and standard, and then duality, we find
E(Z 1,kZ2,ℓ) =δ kℓ
∑
n⩾1
⟨
ιVL−1
V (1U1enυ),Ψ 1

= 0,
since1 U11U2 = 0υ-almost everywhere. The series converges becauseι VL−1
−V (Ψj)belongs to
L2, by Theorem 4.1 applied in positive time. Since such pairings generate theσ-algebras of
AU1 andA U2, these two random connections are independent.
7 Integrating random connections along curves
In order to define the Yang-Mills measure and to verify some of its main properties, we need to
define a random holonomy process which isG-valued. This will be achieved in §8 but before
that, we need to define its logarithmic version which isg-valued. Our goal in this section
is to show thatAψ =ι VL−1
V (Fψ)as defined in Theorem 6.6 satisfies the area law. More
precisely, we need to establish two facts. First, we want to traceAψ on some large class of
curvesγdespite its low regularity. In other words, we want to define a probabilistic analogue
39

===== PAGE 40 =====
of the classical observables from Corollary 5.5, i.e. give a probabilistic meaning of the formal
integrals:
W g
ψ(γ) :=
∫
γ
Aψ.
Recall that, in a deterministic way,Aψ is onlyH−1−(Σ)in Sobolev regularity and that the
current of integration[γ]has regularityH −1/2−(Σ). This makes this integral ill-defined from
the point of view of the classical theory of distributions and there is no wavefront set property
to help us asAψ has isotropicH−1− regularity. Once this issue is settled, we need to compute
the covariance of the resulting Gaussian process in terms of the geometry of the Morse flow
and the surfaceΣ. More precisely, we will express
Φψ(γ) :=E
(‖‖‖W g
ψ(γ)
‖‖‖
2
g
)
in terms of areas with respect to the area formυused in our construction and of domains
built fromγand the gradient flowφ t
f. This is in agreement with Yang–Mills theory where
the law of random holonomies along loops are expressed in terms of areas surrounded by the
loops [80, 81]. Along the way, this procedure defines a new class of quantum observables
W g
ψ(γ)as in Corollary 5.5 which can be thought as logarithmic holonomies.
This long section is organized as follows. First, in §7.1, we introduce some families of
curves along which we aim at definingWg(γ)and random holonomies. We gather some of
their basic properties that will be extensively used in the following paragraphs and sections.
Then, in §7.2, we explain how to integrate the random connections from Theorem 6.6 against
these families of curves. After, we show in §7.3 that the resulting random variables define
ag-valued Brownian motion. Finally, in §7.4, we explain how this construction can also be
carried out for small curves shrinking neara2g+2.
7.1 Geometric preliminaries
7.1.1 Elementary curves

γwhich is transverse to the flow can be decomposed into simple building blocks that we call
elementary curves. We now define this notion of elementary curve and Figure 3 illustrates
the three types of curves we introduce.
Definition 7.1(Elementary curves).Letγ: [0,1]→Σbe aC 1 curve. We say thatγis of
elementary type ifγ([0,1])is transverse 8 to the flow lines ofφt
f,γ((0,1))⊂W u(a1)and if
N(γ) :=♯γ({0,1})∩(Σ\W u(a1))≤1.
Equivalently,γcuts at most once the union∪ ind(a)=1Wu(a)of unstable curves and only at
endpoints.
An elementary curve is said to bepositively (resp. negatively)V-orientedif, for
everyt∈[0,1],γ ′(t)∧V(γ(t))has the same (resp. opposite) orientation compared toΣ. A
V-oriented elementary curve is said to beprimitiveif the map
(s,t)∈(0,1)×R +↦→φ−t
f (γ(s))
is injective.
Definition 7.2(TypeIandIIcurves).Letγbe aV-oriented and primitive elementary curve.
We say thatγis of typeIifN(γ) = 0, i.e.γ([0,1])∩∪ ind(a)=1Wu(a) =∅. Otherwise, we say
thatγis of typeII.
For a curveγof typeII, we denote bya(γ)the only saddle point offsuch thatγ({0,1})∩
Wu(a(γ))̸=∅and we say that it is of typeII± if there exists a sequence(tn)n≥1 in(0,1)such
thatγ(t n)∈W u(a(γ))and such that, fornlarge enough, the orbit ofφ −t
f (γ(tn))enters the
region±x 2≥0(in the Morse chart neara(γ)) fort≥0.
The general principles behind these definitions can be described as follows. First,elemen-
tary curves never meet critical pointsand they are more generally transverse to the flowlines
thanks to the transversality assumption. The orientation assumption is relative to the flow-
lines of the gradient flow while the primitive assumption ensures that there is no overlap inside
the triangle{φ−t
f (γ(s)) : (s,t)∈(0,1)×R +}drawn by the gradient flowlines issued fromγ.
Finally, curves of typeInever meet unstable curves orargmax(f)and, ifγis an elementary
curve of typeII, only one of the endpoints ofγmeets some unstable curve and the rest of the
curve minus this endpoint never intersects any other unstable curve.
With these conventions at hand, we can introduce the following currents of integration:

Definition 7.6.Letγ,˜γbe two curves which are of typeIorII. We say that˜γ≼γif there

7.1.2 Admissible curves
Our purpose is now to define a set of admissible curves for computing random holonomies.

Definition 7.9(TypeIIIcurves).Letγ: [0,1]→Σbe aC 1 curve. We say thatγis of type

admissible curves is made of continuous curves which are piecewiseC1 and the function[▲(γ)] :
Σ→Zis referred to as theindex functionof the closed (but not necessarily exact) curve
[∆(γ)].
Definition 7.11(Index functions).The function[▲(γ)]is called the index of the oriented
curve△(γ).
This definition generalizes the usual notion of Hopf index which measures winding num-
bers. In the case ofΣbeing the Riemann sphere andfbeing the height map, removing
a2 := argmaxfamounts to removing the north pole of the Riemann sphere and to consider
the complex plane. In this case,z∈C↦→[△(γ)](z)∈Zassociates to every curve the usual
45

===== PAGE 46 =====
Figure 6: Index for various Lipschitz curves.
Hopf index ofγ. See [39, Prop 4.16 p. 42] and we refer to Figure 6 to illustrate examples of
Hopf indices for various closed curves.
When each curveγj of typeIorIIused to defineγis included in a level set off, we shall
say thatγis ofTetris type. These are typically the kind of curves appearing in the lattice
approximation strategy that can be found in [24]. We refer to Figure 7 where we give examples
of Tetris curves. As for the elementary case, we also introduce the notion ofV-oriented curves
with respect to the vector fieldV.
Definition 7.12(AdmissibleV-oriented curves).Letγ: [0,1]→Σbe an admissible curve.
We say thatγisV-oriented with respect toVif, for every0≤s < t≤1,[▲(γ([s,t]))]has
the same sign.
Observe that, along segments[s,t]whereγcorresponds to a typeIII-curve, the value of
[▲(γ([0,τ]))]is constant.
Definition 7.13(Admissible primitive curves).Letγ: [0,1]→Σbe an admissible and
V-oriented curve. We say thatγis primitive with respect toVif, for every0≤t≤1,
|[▲(γ([0,t]))]|≤1.
In other words, it means that the triangle drawn by the curveγand the flow lines ofV
has no overlap. Both notions coincide with the ones used for elementary curves.
We conclude this discussion with the following key lemma for our probabilistic construc-
tions.

7.1.3 Approximable currents
We now enlarge the above class of curves we are dealing with to allow for more general
observables of the type
∫
γAψ at the quantum level. We will call the corresponding currents
approximable. This class contains all possible curvesγon which we will be able to define the
observables
∫
γAin a probabilistic manner. We consider the space of admissible curves from
definition 7.12 and these define currents that we can endow with the weak topology. Recall
that each admissible curve is oriented from0to1and thus defines a current of integration
[γ]of finite mass.
Definition 7.15(Currents with good approximation properties).A currentTis said to be
approximable if
•there exists a sequence of admissible curves(γn)n⩾1 such that the sequence of currents
[γn]converge weakly to the currentT(for the weak topology ofD′,1(Σ)),
•the corresponding sequence of index functions([▲(γn)])n∈N is Cauchy inL2
υ(Σ).
In the following, we will denote by Ind(T)the limit of[▲(γn)]inL 2
υ(Σ). Let us give two
illustrative examples9 to give the reader a sense of how large this class is.
Example 7.16.We work in[0,10]×[0,10]that we view as a flowbox of our gradient flow. For
everyn, consider the squareS n whose vertices are(2−n,2−n),(2−n +n−α,2−n),(2−n,2−n +
n−α),(2−n +n−α,2−n +n−α)forα> 1
2 andγ n is the straight segment connecting(2−n,2−n)
and(2−n−1,2−n−1). Then, eachSn is admissible and one can show that
T=
∑
n⩾0
[Sn] + [γn]
is approximable in the above sense. Forα>1, the current has finite mass since the union of
squares has finite length but we see that, forα∈(1
2,1], the mass ofTis infinite.
9Another example can be found in [65, fig 4.3.4 p. 42] which yields a setEof finite area but with infinite
perimeter.
48

===== PAGE 49 =====
Example 7.17.We work on the planeR 2. Consider a collection of circlesCn all oriented
counterclockwise of radiusn−α centered at(0,n−α),α > 1
2 which are tangent at one given
point. Then the corresponding currentT:=∑

Forα >1, the current has finite mass since the union of circles has finite length but we see
that, forα∈( 1
2,1], the mass ofTis infinite.
Our definition of approximable currents can be related with classical concepts from geo-
metric measure theory. In fact, in the above definition and as can be seen from the above
examples, we do not even assumeTto be an integral current meaning a rectifiable current
of dimension1with integer multiplicity whose boundary is also rectifiable with integer mul-
tiplicity [65, p. 39–41]. To ensure integral properties, one needs to ensure extra boundedness
assumptions:
Lemma 7.18(Criterion for integrability).If we are given a sequence of admissible curves
(γn)n such that the length of(△(γ n))n is uniformly bounded and such that the sequence
([▲(γn)])n is Cauchy inL2 and[△(γ n)]→Tweakly inD ′. ThenTis an integral current.
Since we are only dealing with1-dimensional objects, this simply means that∂Tis a finite
sum of integration currents on points, so the support of∂Tis just a finite number of points.
Proof.By assumption,[△(γ n)]is a bounded sequence for the mass norm and also note that
[▲(γn)]is bounded inL 2(Σ). Thus it is bounded inL 1(Σ)sinceΣhas finite area. By
definition, the massM([▲γ n])of the integer valued function[▲(γ n)]is nothing but theL 1
norm∥[▲(γn)]∥L1(Σ,υ). Sowehaveasequence([▲(γ n)])n⩾1 ofintegralcurrentswhosemassand
boundary mass remain bounded and such that([▲(γn)])n⩾1 converges inL2(Σ)(by definition
since it is Cauchy inL2) hence inL1(Σ)(becauseΣhas finite area) and for the weak topology.
In other words, the♭–norm [65, p. 41] of the sequence([△(γn)])n remains bounded. By the
closure theorem [65, item (4) Thm 5.4 p. 62], the sequence([▲(γn)])n converges to some
integral current denoted by Ind(T)∈I2(Σ)which is an integer valued function inL1 whose
boundary∂Ind(T) =Tdefines an integral currentT∈I 1(Σ). In particularTis rectifiable
of finite length.

8 Gaussian random holonomies
In Section 7, we defined areparametrizedversion of a Brownian motion on the Lie algebra
through the definition of the stochastic processt↦→Wψ,γ(t)associated with the white noise
ξψ. Recall that this requires some primitive properties of the curveγ– see Section 7 for
definitions and statements. In view of constructing the Yang-Mills measure and its holonomy
processes, we need to solve the stochastic differential equation associated withWψ,γ. This
solution will represent the holonomy ofAψ alongγ.
All along this section, we letγbe a primitive curve that we suppose without typeIII-
components. We will also suppose that the area functional
Aγ :t∈[0,1]↦→
∫
Σ
[▲(γ([0,t]))]υ
is increasing (the decreasing case follows by reversing the time parametrization of the curve).
In fact, we will make a small abuse of notations and allow the curveγto be formally reduced
a2g+2 =argmax(f)in order to include the stochastic process introduced in §7.4. Recall that,
in the setting of §7.4,[▲(γ([0,t]))]is replaced by the limit function[▲ 0(t)]. In each case,
it follows from Lemmas 7.14 and 7.24 thatAγ belongs to every Sobolev spaceW 1,p([0,1])
with1≤p <∞and that it isC 1 except at finitely many points whereA′
γ has singularities
of logarithmic type. These singularities correspond to the points of the curveγwhere we
switch from an elementary piece to another. They are indeed of logarithmic type when one
has elementary curves of typeII, meaning the points whereγintersects someW u(a)with
ind(a) = 1.
Remark 8.1.In §7.4, we also used the conventionW0(t)whenγis formally the blow-up of
argmax(f) =a 2g+2. In order to alleviate the notations, we use all along this section the same
convention for both settings, i.e. we setγ= 0when we deal with the blow-up case.
Remark 8.2.All along this section, we also make the assumption thatψ≡1as in later
applications, we intend to apply the results from this Section withξU =1 Uξ, whereUcontains
the support of[▲(γ([0,t]))]for allt∈[0,1]. In that case, one hasW 1,γ =W 1U,γ. In particular,
the stochastic process depends only onξU whereUis the support of[▲(γ([0,1]))],the latter
being equal to[▲(γ)]asγisV-oriented. In order to alleviate notations, we set
Wγ :=W 1,γ.
Also from Remark 6.5, one finds that, ifγand˜γverifies[▲(γ([0,t]))][▲(˜γ([0,t′]))] = 0for all
t,t′∈[0,1], then the two stochastic processesWγ andW ˜γare independent.
This Section is organized as follows. First, in §8.1, we review a few properties of harmonic
analysis on Lie groups. In particular, we introduce the heat kernelpt onG. Then, in §8.2, we
define the holonomy of a random connection along a fixed curveγand gather its main prop-
erties in Theorem 8.4. Finally, in §8.3, we describe the Markov properties of this holonomy
process.

8.2 Local gaussian random holonomies
If we denote byFγ(t)theσ-algebra generated 10 by(W γ(s))0≤s≤t and containing all the set
ofP-measure0, it is by construction independent of theσ-algebra generated by(W γ(s)−
Wγ(t))s≥t (see e.g. Remark 8.2). The family ofσ-algebrat↦→Fγ(t)is a filtration (or also
non-anticipating), and the continuous processWγ is a (local) martingale with respect to this
filtration [69, Ch. IV, Def.1.5] in the sense that
∀0≤s≤t≤1,E(W γ(t)|Fγ(s)) =W γ(s),
which follows directly from Lemma 7.22. In order to define our random holonomies associated
with the curveγ, we solve the following stochastic differential equation:

This equation is the stochastic analogue of the classical parallel transport equation (1.1).
See Appendix A for a brief reminder on Itô’s integral for reparametrized brownian motions.

the different statements in this theorem. Here, the main difference with references like [38] is
that the derivative of the reparametrizationA′

many logarithmic singularities.

Remark 8.7.Our holonomy process is well-defined for any curveγwhich is primitive without
type III components. In this case,Wγ(t)is in fact a reparametrization of the standard Brow-
nian motion as we have seen above. Hence, it is amenable to the standard theory of stochastic
differential equations [69, Ch. IX] thanks to the local martingale properties ofWγ. In fact, we
could define holonomies for more general curves that are concatenation of primitive curves as
in this paragraph by taking products of these local holonomies (with the holonomy being equal
to the identity along flow lines) as we did in the previous proof. This will be the content of
§ 10.1 where we will also include the contribution of the unstable manifolds(Wu(a))ind(a)=1.

8.3 Markov properties of gaussian random holonomies

mechanism that we call abelianization in law in its most elementary form. For the random
holonomies of Theorem 8.4, this property reads:

9 Definition of the Yang–Mills measure by conditioning
We will now gather the results of the previous sections to define the Yang-Mills measure on
spaces of connections but also on a large set of observables. In that manner, we will achieve
the main goals of this article and prove Theorems 2.3 and 2.4 from the introduction. This
is organized in several steps. First, we define the free boundary Yang-Mills measure where
the contribution of the white noise (encoding the curvature) and of the unstable manifolds
([Wu(a)])ind(a)=1 (encoding the topology ofΣ) are independent. This is the content of §9.1.
In order to define a proper Yang-Mills measure capturing the topological properties of the
surface, we need to couple the information from the white noise with the information coming
from the1-dimensional unstable manifolds. To do that, we introduce random holonomies
near the maximum and gather some of their properties in §9.2. Once this is settled, we
discussin §9.3thequantum observablesweaimatintegrating againsttheYang–Millsmeasure.
Then, we define the Yang-Mills measure for large set of observables by conditioning the free
boundary measure with the requirement that holonomies near the maximum are roughly
equal to the identity. This is done in §9.4 which contains the main Theorem of this section,
namely Theorem 9.16. Gathered with classical tools from measure theory and functional
analysis [40, 52], this theorem implies Theorems 2.3 and 2.4 except for the law of random
holonomies that will be described in §10. More precisely, while Theorem 2.3 is a direct
consequence of Theorem 9.16 (up the laws of random holonomies), the proof of Theorem 2.4
requires more work which is done in §9.5.
9.1 The free boundary Yang–Mills measure
In order to define the Yang-Mills measure, we start by defining thefree boundary Yang–
Mills measurewhere we do not put any coupling between the components of the connection
Acoming from the white noiseξand from the one coming from the unstable components
([Wu(a)])ind(a)=1.

This generalizes Definition 2.5 which only defined a measure on connections while we
allow here a larger set of observables. The goal of this section is to proceed to appropriate
conditioning of this measure to define the Yang–Mills measure on a large set of observables
including for instance random connections and random holonomies. In view of Theorems 2.1
and 2.2, we set

9.2.1 Topological preliminaries
Recall that there exist consecutive “angles”0≤t1<t 2<...<t 4g−1 <t 4g≤1such that the
piece of curver↦→(rcos(2πtj),rsin(2πt j))is a piece of unstable manifolds corresponding to a
critical pointaof index1. We denote bya(tj)the corresponding critical point and we observe
that each critical point of index1appears exactly twice. Equivalently, for every critical point
aof index1, there exist1≤j <k≤4gsuch thata=a(t j) =a(t k). This allows us to define
the following permutation ofZ/4gZ:
ϱf :j∈Z/4gZ↦→k+ 1∈Z/4gZ,
wherekis the unique integer such thata(tj) =a(t k). The following holds:

9.3 Admissible observables
Before stating the main result of this section, we will discuss the main observables we want
to integrate on our probability space. Roughly speaking, we aim at covering (measurable)
functions of the following random variables:
1.⟨A,ψ⟩for anyψ∈Ω 1
c(Σ\{a 2g+2},g);
2. random holonomiesHol(γ)along admissible closed curvesγ;
3.W ψ,T whereψ∈L ∞(Σ)and whereTis an approximable current.
The last two items can be viewed as the probabilistic analogues of the classical observables
from Corollary 5.5, namely Holγ(A)and
∫
γA(whenT= [γ]).
Remark 9.13.Note that, for a general approximable currentT, the unstable component
⟨T,Au⟩is a priori ill-defined even if the Gaussian partWψ,T :=⟨T,A N⟩was properly defined
in a probabilistic way. A manner to handle this problem is to make an extra assumption on
the wavefront properties ofTor to suppose thatTis compactly supported inW u(a1)as in
Corollary 5.5. In the case whereT= [γ]for some admissibleγ, the transversality assumption
involved in the definition of admissible curves ensures that⟨T,Au⟩is well-defined.

the measuresP free

property from Theorem 8.10. Gathering all these elements will lead us to the proper definition
of the Yang–Mills measure (under Morse gauge) as we will now explain it.

9.5 Definition of the Yang–Mills measure
We will now explain how to construct the measure from Theorem 2.4 as a by-product of
Theorem 9.16. We begin by defining the distributional spaces adapted to our dynamical
constructions in view of applying Prokhorov Theorem. In fact, after singular conditioning, we
lost information on the connectionAat the critical pointa2g+2 = argmax(f)and therefore it
is natural to work in functional spaces of the pointed spaceΣ\{a2g+2}. This explains why it
is more natural to work with a weighted version of the Sobolev spaceH−1−κ
loc (Σ\{a 2g+2})used
so far. In some sense, the weight measures the growth of the distribution when we approach
a2g+2. This is reminiscent of the weighted Lebesgue spaces used in Theorem 4.1. We are very
grateful to Elias Nohra for suggesting to introduce also weighted Sobolev spaces here rather
than working on the larger spaceD′(Σ\{a 2g+2}).

priori non uniquely) as a distribution inD′ (Σ,T∗Σ⊗g)by the Hahn–Banach Theorem. We
refer the reader to [17, section 2 p. 157–163], [1, Section 5] and specially to the [17, Example

10 Random holonomies for the Yang–Mills measure
In this section, we define random holonomies for general admissible curves (see §10.1) and
we explain how to integrate these quantities with respect to the Yang-Mills functional as
it appears in Theorem 9.16. This is explained in §10.2 and we give in §10.3 an example
of computation of this law for small loops with respect to the free boundary measure and
in §10.4 another example for the Yang–Mills measure. Once these definitions are settled, the
main result of this Section is Theorem 10.7 where we compute the law for random holonomies
along curves which are the boundaries of small admissible disks contained in flow boxes of
the gradient flowφt
f used to construct the Yang–Mills measure. As we shall see, the law for
random holonomy along such curves is independent of the choice of the Morse function and
it matches with the results from the literature for instance as [57, subsection 1.8.2 p. 15].
Actually, such a formula would immediately follow if we could prove that our Yang–Mills
measure recovers the more general Driver–Sengupta formulas [57, equations(1.1),(1.2),(1.3)
p. 6]. We leave the proof of such a general formula as a question for future investigations.
10.1 Random holonomies for admissible curves
Given an elementary curveγ, we define
γ(t) =γ(1−t), t∈[0,1].
Withthisconvention, wecanintroducetheelementary(random)holonomyofAalongγ. More
precisely, thanks to Theorem 8.4 and to Lemma 8.6, we would like to define the holonomy of
γwith respect toA=A(ω,(b a))as the product of the elementary holonomies. Yet, we need
to take into account the contribution of the unstable manifolds([Wu(a)])ind(a)=1 and this can
be handled in the following way.
Definition 10.1.Letγbe an elementary curve. The holonomy alongγof the random con-
nectionAis defined as follows:
84

===== PAGE 85 =====
1. Ifγis of typeIII, thenHol(γ) = Id.
2. Ifγis of typeIorIIand ift∈[0,1]↦→A γ(t)is increasing, then
Hol(γ) :=
{
gγ(1)ifγ(0)∈W u(a1),
gγ(1)bε(γ)
a ifγ(0)∈W u(a)for someaof index1,
whereg γ(1)is the random holonomy defined in Theorem 8.4 and whereε(γ) = 1if the
flowline fromγ(0)toa 2g+2 has the same orientation asWu(a)andε(γ) =−1otherwise.
3. Ifγis of typeIorIIand ift∈[0,1]↦→A γ(t)is decreasing, then
Hol(γ) :=Hol( γ)−1.
This leads to the definition of random holonomy along a general admissible curve.
Definition 10.2.Letγ=γ 1⋆(γ 2⋆(...⋆γ J)...)be an admissible curve. Then, one defines
the holonomy of the random connectionAalongγas
Hol(γ) :=Hol(γ J)...Hol(γ 2)Hol(γ1).
Thanks to Lemma 8.6, this definition is independent of the decomposition ofγin elemen-
tary curves.

10.5 Holonomies along boundaries of small disks with no critical points
We will now compute the law of random holonomies along continuous and piecewiseC1 curves
γ: [0,1]→Σwhich are the concatenation of elementary curves and which are theboundary of
a small open set homeomorphic to a disk not containing any critical point. Thesecorrespondto
the admissible disks from Theorem 2.3. More precisely, we will make the following simplifying
assumptions:
88

===== PAGE 89 =====
Figure 12: Example for (H1).
•γ 1,γ 2 : [0,1]→ΣareC 1 curves such thatγj([0,1])does not contain any critical point;
•γ 1 andγ 2 are primitive and verify thatt∈[0,1]↦→Aj(t) :=A γj(t)is increasing for
eachj∈{1,2};
•γ 1(0) =γ 2(0)andγ 1(1) =γ 2(1).
Our goal is to compute the random holonomy along the piecewiseC1 curve
γ=γ 1⋆γ2.(10.2)

assumption that

We end this section by mentioning an open question, namely recover the full Driver–
Sengupta formula at some level of generality close to what is formulated in the work of
Lévy [57, Eq.(1.1),(1.2),(1.3)p. 6], [59, p. 289] using our version of the closed Yang–Mills
measure. Our calculations for small loops are a first step towards this direction. Since we
believe this might be a difficult combinatorial problem, it is possible that one would need to
modify the present setting to make such proofs natural.
11 AbelianYang–Mills(Maxwell)theoryonsurfacesviaMorse
gauge.
The goal of the present section is to discuss how our quantization works in theG=U(1)case
as well as to describe certain subtleties of the inducedYM2 measure related to topologies of
line bundles.
11.1 U(1)-principal bundle and associated complex Line bundles
We recall the correspondence between principal circle bundles and line bundles. LetMbe
a manifold andπ:P→Mbe a principalU(1)bundle. Letρ:U(1)→C ∗ be a unitary
representation, then we can construct the associated complex line bundlesL:=P× ρ C:=
P×C/∼, where the equivalence relation is(p,z)∼(pg,ρ(g) −1z), g∈U(1). The one-
dimensional unitary representations ofU(1)are classified by integersn∈Z. Givenn∈Z,
defineρ n :U(1)→C ∗,ρn(eiθ) =e inθ,n∈Z. Then we obtain the associated complex line
bundleL n :=P× ρn C:=P×C/∼. Also observe that the bundleL n can be realized as the
n-th tensor power ofL1, namelyLn =L⊗n
1 .
Conversely, let(L,h)be a Hermitian line bundle, thenS(L) :={u∈L:|u| h = 1}
is aU(1)-principal bundle with the action given bye iθ·u=e iθu.And we have natural
isomorphisms:
S(L)× ρ C→L,[u,z]↦→zu
P→S(P× ρ C),u↦→[u,1].
A connection onPis a Lie algebra valued one formA∈Ω 1(P,iR). Given any connectionA0
onP, the space of connections onPis an affine space modeled onΩ 1(M,R)given explicitly
byA(P) ={A 0 +iπ∗α:α∈Ω 1(M,R)}. A connection (or covariant derivative) ofLis a
linear map∇: Γ 0(M,L)→Ω 1(M,L)satisfying∇(fs) =df⊗s+f∇sfor anys∈C ∞(M,L)
andf∈C ∞(M). Given any connection∇0 onL, then we have the space of connections ofL
is given byA(L) ={∇ 0 +α:α∈Ω 1(M,R)}. We summarize what we just described in the
following:

tation ofU(1). There is a1−1correspondence space of connections onPand the covariant
derivatives on an associated vector bundleL. In particular, the curvatures are the same via
this correspondence.

We give an example in genus0where we build a non trivial line bundle onCP1≃S 2.
Consider the Hopf bundleP=S 3 :={(z 1,z 2)∈C 2||z 1|2 +|z 2|2 = 1}onCP 1. This model

that the standard representationρ1 :U(1)→C ∗,ρ 1(eiθ)(z) =e iθzdefines the associated line

connections of arbitrary Chern number inZ.
Now we will consider only connections on a complex line bundleLon a compact surface
Σinstead of the principal bundleP.
From connections on non trivial line bundles to singular connections on the trivial
line bundle.Our construction of the Yang–Mills measure suggests a change of perspective
on how to deal with connections on non-trivial line bundlesL→Σ. Starting from any non
trivial line bundleL→Σ, for instance when we constructed the free boundary measure, we
first remove the critical pointa2g+2 = argmax(f)and then consider the restriction of the line
bundleL→Σ\{a 2g+2}. However by doing so topology is lost since it is well–known that the
restricted bundleL→Σ\{a 2g+2}becomes trivial.
Then in our probabilistic constructions, all the objects live onΣ\argmax(f)and can be
extended as currents on the wholeΣ. A natural question is: how can we recover non triviality
of line bundles onΣ(capture the topology) by working only with connections living on the
pointed surfaceΣ\argmax(f)?

11.2 Morse gauge for nontrivialU(1)bundle
In this final short section, we derive formulas in the Abelian case as a toy model of the
machinery developed in the present paper and we also explain how our formalism applied to
abeliancaseyieldsaYang–Millsmeasureonconnectionswhichcontaintopologicalinformation
in the sense of Lemma 11.4. This should indicate how one could attack the case of nontrivial
bundles modulo some extra work. In the case whereG=U(1)≃S 1, the theory developed
above is indeed slightly simpler to deal with even if several technical aspects remain unchanged
(e.g. application of Theorem 4.1 to prove Theorem 2.2 or conditioning at the maximum of
f).

A Stochasticdifferentialequationsforcontinuousreparametriza-
tions of the Brownian motion
In this appendix, we review Itô’s calculus and resolution of stochastic differential equations
when one considers reparametrization of the Brownian motion by anincreasingmapA∈
C0([0,1],R +)which is also of classC 1 except at finitely many points(Tj)j∈J in[0,1]where
A′(t) =O(|ln|t−T j||). The purpose of this appendix is to describe the basic theory for
readers less familiar with stochastic calculus who will find here some material and references
on this topic. In particular, we explain that, despite the low regularities properties ofA, Itô’s
integration (and its application to stochastic differential equations) indeed makes sense [69,
Ch. IV].
Remark A.1.For the1-dimensional case, we follow the presentation of [36, Ch. 4,5] while,
for the case of compact Lie groups, we rather follow [38, Ch. 6-7]. See also [69, Ch. IV] for
a presentation of stochastic integration in a general setting handling the integration of more
generalW(and thusA). Finally, discussion on stochastic differential equations is a mixture
of these three references.
More precisely, we proceed in two steps. First, we review what Itô’s integral is for a
reparametrized Brownian motion. Then, we discuss the resolution of stochastic differential
equations for such stochastic processes. All along this appendix and onceAis fixed, we
suppose thatW:t∈[0,1]↦→Ris a stochastic process that is almost surelyC β([0,1])and
that satisfies the following properties:
1.W(0) = 0;
2. for all0≤s≤t,W(t)−W(s)follows a normal law with variance
∫t
s A′(τ)dτ;
3. for allp⩾1and for all0 =t 0 < t1 < t2 < ... < tn⩽1,W(t 1),W(t 2)−W(t 1),...,
W(tp)−W(t p−1)are independent random variables.
Recall also that under these assumptions, one has
∀s,t∈[0,1],E(W(t)W(s)) = min{A(t),A(s)}.
A.1 Background on Itô’s integrals
Following the lines of [36, Ch. 4], one can first verify that
lim

Itô’s integral and, following [69, Ch. IV], this is referred to as the quadratic variation of the
process. From this, one can deduce that:
lim

Finally, we record the following lemma stating thatX(t)has the properties of a Brownian

Lemma A.8.LetX(t)be the solution to(A.8). Then, the following holds.
1. for everyp⩾1and for every0 =t 0 < t1 < t2 < ... < tp≤1,X(t 1),X(t 1)−1X(t2),
...,X(t p−1)−1X(tp)are independent;
2. for all0⩽s⩽t⩽1,X(s) −1X(t)has the same distribution asX(t−s);
3. for everyhinG,X(t)andhX(t)h −1 have the same distribution.
Proof.Forthefirstproperty, wefixs∈[0,1]andweobservethat(X(s) −1X(t))t≥s solves(A.8)
with initial conditionX(s) =Id. As the solution to this equation is unique according to
Theorem A.4, it is measurable with respect to theσ-algebra generated by(W(t)−W(s))t≥s.
In particular, from the properties of theg-valued Brownian, it is independent of the one
generated byW(s). Hence,X(s) −1X(t)andX(s)are independent. The casep >2follows
the same argument.
For the second property, it follows from Corollary A.7 combined with the above discussion.
Finally, the last property can be viewed as a consequence of the invariance of the heat
kernel by conjugation – see §8.1 for explicit expressions.
